| BY WILLIAM SHAKESPEARE AND | OTHERS |
| FIRST | heard of in the Stationers’ Register for I December 1595, The Reign of [[archetypes/king\|King]] Edward the Third was published anonymously in the following year, with the statement that it had been ‘sundry times played about the [[motifs/city\|City]] of [[settings/london\|London]]’. As was usual, there are no act and scene divisions; we divide it only into scenes. It could have been written at any [[motifs/time\|time]] between the Armada of 1588 and 1595. Like other plays of this period, including Shakespeare’s I [[characters/henry-vi\|Henry VI]], [[characters/richard-ii\|Richard II]], and [[characters/king-john\|King John]], it is composed entirely in verse, much of it formal and rhetorical in style. Shakespeare seems at least to have known the play, since a historical error placing [[archetypes/king\|King]] David of Scotland among Edward’s prisoners at Calais (10.40-56, 18.63.1) occurs also in [[characters/henry-v\|Henry V]] (1.2.160-2). The play’s omission from the First Folio is good presumptive evidence against Shakespeare’s sole authorship. It was, however, attributed to him in a totally unreliable catalogue of 1656; better worth taking seriously is the attribution to Shakespeare by Edward Capell, expressed in 1760. Since then various scholars have proposed that Shakespeare wrote at least the scenes involving the [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|Countess of Salisbury]] (Scene 2, Scene 3). When the [[settings/oxford\|Oxford]] edition first appeared, its editors remarked that ‘if we had attempted a thorough reinvestigation of candidates for inclusion in the early dramatic canon, it would have begun with [[characters/edward-iii\|Edward III]]’ (Textual Companion, p. 137). Since then intensive application of stylometric and other tests of authorship, along with an increased willingness to acknowledge that Shakespeare collaborated with other writers, especially early and late in his career, has strengthened the case for including it among the collected works. We believe, however, that Shakespeare was responsible only for Scene 2 (from the entrance of [[characters/edward-iii\|Edward III]]) and Scene 3, and for Scene 12 (which includes a [[characters/hamlet\|Hamlet]]-like meditation on the inevitability of [[motifs/death\|death]]), and possibly Scene 13, and that one or more other authors wrote the rest of the play.<br>The play’s treatment of [[concepts/history\|history]], deriving principally from Lord Berners’s [[forms/translation\|translation]] (1535) of Froissart’s Chronicles, is loose. As with [[characters/henry-v\|Henry V]], the opening episode shows the English [[archetypes/king\|king]] seeking reassurance about his claims to the throne of France. Lorraine’s subsequent demand that Edward swear allegiance to the French [[archetypes/king\|king]] meets with derision. Attention turns to England’s relations with Scotland, where [[archetypes/king\|King]] David, France’s ally, has besieged the [[settings/castle\|castle]] of Roxburgh, imprisoning the [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|Countess of Salisbury]]. Edward instructs his son Edward (Ned) the Black Prince to raise troops against France; Edward himself will march against the Scots. At Roxburgh the [[archetypes/king\|King]] rescues and attempts to seduce the Countess, who is also desired by [[archetypes/king\|King]] David and Sir William Douglas. In the principal scenes ascribed to Shakespeare, the enraptured [[archetypes/king\|King]] expresses his passion in attractively lyrical verse recalling that of The Two Gentlemen of [[settings/verona\|Verona]]. He attempts to persuade the [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|Earl of Warwick]], the Countess’s father, to further his suit, but the Countess, virtuous (and married), repudiates his adulterous desires, threatening to kill herself if he persists. Penitent, he reverts to the French conflict. This, presented in episodes of ambitious rhetoric rather than of violent action, reaches its first climax in young Edward’s conquest over the [[archetypes/king\|King]] of Bohemia, for which his father knights him. Edward’s [[characters/queen\|queen]], Philippa, who with her followers has overcome the Scots, joins him, and persuades him to show [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]] to the burghers of the besieged town of Calais. Young Edward, believed dead, is revealed as the conqueror of the French, and the play ends with a jingoistic English triumph. It has had a few modern productions, including one by the Royal Shakespeare Company in |

| [[characters/queen\|QUEEN]] | PHILIPPA, his wife<br>Edward, PRINCE OF WALES, their eldest son<br>The [[characters/earl-of-salisbury\|EARL OF SALISBURY]]<br>The [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]], his wife<br>The [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]], the Countess’s father<br>Sir William de MONTAGUE, Salisbury’s nephew<br>The EARL OF DERBY<br>Sir James AUDLEY<br>Henry, Lord PERCY<br>John COPLAND, an esquire, later knighted<br>LODOWICK, [[archetypes/king\|King]] Edward’s secretary<br>Two SQUIRES<br>A HERALD to [[archetypes/king\|King]] Edward from the Prince of Wales Four heralds who bear the Prince of Wales’s armour Soldiers<br><br>Allied with the English<br>Robert, COMTE D’ARTOIS and [[characters/earl-of-richmond\|Earl of Richmond]]<br>Jean, COMTE DE MONTFORT, later Duc de Bretagne |
| GOBIN | de Grace, a French Prisoner<br><br>The French<br>Jean II de Valois, [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]]<br>Prince Charles, Jean’s eldest son, Duc de Normandie, the [[characters/dauphin\|DAUPHIN]] |
| PRINCE | PHILIPPE, Jean’s younger son<br>The DUC DE LORRAINE<br>VILLIERS, a prisoner sent as an envoy by the [[characters/earl-of-salisbury\|Earl of Salisbury]] to the [[characters/dauphin\|Dauphin]]<br>The CAPTAIN OF CALAIS<br>Another FRENCH CAPTAIN<br>A MARINER<br>Three HERALDS to the Prince of Wales from the [[characters/king-of-france\|King of France]], the [[characters/dauphin\|Dauphin]] and Prince Philippe<br>Six POOR MEN, residents of Calais<br>Six SUPPLICANTS, wealthy merchants and citizens of Calais<br>Five other FRENCHMEN<br>A FRENCIIWOMAN with two children<br>Soldiers<br><br>Allied with the French<br>The [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF BOHEMIA<br>A POLISH CAPTAIN<br>Polish and Muscovite soldiers<br>David II, [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF SCOTLAND<br>Sir William DOUGLAS<br>Two Scottish MESSENGERS<br><br>The Reign of [[archetypes/king\|King]] Edward the Third<br><br>Sc. 1 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[archetypes/king\|King]] Edward, the Earl of Derby, the [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|Earl of Warwick]], Edward Prince of Wales, Lord Audley and the Comte d’Artois |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Robert of Artois, banished though thou be<br>From France thy native country, yet with us<br>Thou shalt retain as great a seigniory:<br>For we create thee [[characters/earl-of-richmond\|Earl of Richmond]] here.<br>And now go forwards with our pedigree:<br>Who next succeeded [[archetypes/king\|King]] Philippe of Beau? |
| COMTE D’ARTOIS | Three sons of his, which all successively<br>Did sit upon their father’s regal throne,<br>Yet died and left no issue of their loins. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | But was my mother sister unto those? |
| COMTE D’ARTOIS | She was, my lord, and only Isabel<br>Was all the daughters that this Philippe had,<br>Whom afterward your father took to wife.<br>And from the fragrant [[motifs/garden\|garden]] of her womb<br>Your gracious [[concepts/self\|self]], the flower of Europe’s [[concepts/hope\|hope]],<br>Derived is inheritor to France.<br>But note the rancour of rebellious minds:<br>When thus the lineage of Beau was out<br>The French obscured your mother’s privilege<br>And, though she were the next of [[motifs/blood\|blood]], proclaimed<br>Jean of the house of Valois now their [[archetypes/king\|king]].<br>The reason was, they say, the realm of France<br>Replete with princes of great parentage<br>Ought not admit a governor to rule<br>Except he be descended of the male.<br>And that’s the special ground of their contempt<br>Wherewith they study to exclude your grace. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | But they shall find that forged ground of theirs<br>To be but dusty heaps of brittle sand. |
| COMTE D’ARTOIS | Perhaps it will be thought a heinous thing<br>That I, a Frenchman, should discover this.<br>But heaven I call to record of my vows:<br>It is not hate nor any private wrong,<br>But [[motifs/love\|love]] unto my country and the right<br>Provokes my tongue thus lavish in report.<br>You are the lineal watchman of our peace,<br>And Jean of Valois indirectly climbs.<br>What then should subjects but embrace their [[archetypes/king\|king]]?<br>Ah, wherein may our duty more be seen<br>Than striving to rebate a [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]]’s [[concepts/pride\|pride]]<br>And place thee, the true shepherd of our commonwealth? |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | This counsel, Artois, like to fruitful showers,<br>Hath added growth unto my dignity,<br>And by the fiery vigour of thy words<br>Hot [[concepts/courage\|courage]] is engendered in my breast,<br>Which heretofore was raked in ignorance<br>But now doth mount with golden wings of fame<br>And will approve fair Isabel’s descent,<br>Able to yoke their stubborn necks with steel<br>That spurn against my sovereignty in France. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound a horn |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | A messenger. Lord Audley, know from whence. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a messenger, the Duc de Lorraine |
| AUDLEY | The Duke of Lorraine, having crossed the seas,<br>Entreats he may have conference with your highness. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Admit him, lords, that we may hear the news.<br>(To Lorraine) Say, Duke of Lorraine, wherefore art thou come? 55 |
| DUC DE LORRAINE | The most renowned prince, [[archetypes/king\|King]] Jean of France,<br>Doth greet thee, Edward, and by me commands<br>That, forsomuch as by his liberal gift<br>The Guienne dukedom is entailed to thee,<br>Thou do him lowly homage for the same.<br>And for that purpose, here I summon thee<br>Repair to France within these forty days<br>That there, according as the custom is,<br>Thou mayst be sworn true liegeman to our [[archetypes/king\|king]];<br>Or else thy title in that province dies<br>And he himself will repossess the place. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | See how occasion laughs me in the face!<br>No sooner minded to prepare for France<br>But straight I am invited—nay, with threats,<br>Upon a penalty, enjoined to come!<br>‘Twere but a childish part to say him nay.<br>Lorraine, return this answer to thy lord:<br>I mean to visit him as he requests.<br>But how? Not servilely disposed to bend,<br>But like a conqueror to make him bow.<br>His lame unpolished shifts are come to light,<br>And [[concepts/truth\|truth]] hath pulled the vizard from his face<br>That set a gloss upon his arrogance.<br>Dare he command a fealty in me?<br>Tell him the [[motifs/crown\|crown]] that he usurps is mine,<br>And where he sets his foot he ought to kneel.<br>’Tis not a petty dukedom that I claim<br>But all the whole dominions of the realm<br>Which if, with grudging, he refuse to yield<br>I’ll take away those borrowed plumes of his,<br>And send him naked to the wilderness. |
| DUC DE LORRAINE | Then, Edward, here, in spite of all thy lords,<br>I do pronounce defiance to thy face. |
| PRINCE OF WALES | Defiance, Frenchman? We rebound it back<br>Even to the [[characters/bottom\|bottom]] of thy master’s throat!<br>And, be it spoke with reverence of the [[archetypes/king\|King]],<br>My gracious father, and these other lords,<br>I hold thy message but as scurrilous,<br>And him that sent thee like the lazy drone<br>Crept up by stealth unto the eagle’s nest,<br>From whence we’ll shake him with so rough a [[motifs/storm\|storm]]<br>As others shall be warned by his harm. |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | (to Lorraine)<br>Bid him leave off the [[motifs/lion\|lion]]’s case he wears<br>Lest, meeting with the [[motifs/lion\|lion]] in the field,<br>He chance to tear him piecemeal for his [[concepts/pride\|pride]]. |
| COMTE D’ARTOIS | (to Lorraine)<br>The soundest counsel I can give his grace<br>Is to surrender ere he be constrained.<br>A voluntary mischief hath less scorn<br>Than when reproach with violence is borne. |
| DUC DE LORRAINE | Regenerate traitor, viper to the place<br>Where thou wast fostered in thine infancy!<br>Bear’st thou a part in this conspiracy? |
| *(didascalia)* | Lorraine draws his sword |
| *(didascalia)* | [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD drawing his sword |
| DUC DE LORRAINE | Lorraine, behold the sharpness of this steel:<br>Fervent [[motifs/desire\|desire]] that sits against my heart<br>Is far more thorny-pricking than this blade<br>That, with the [[motifs/nightingale\|nightingale]], I shall be scarred<br>As oft as I dispose myself to rest<br>Until my colours be displayed in France.<br>This is thy final answer. So be gone. |
| DUC DE LORRAINE | It is not that, nor any English brave,<br>Afflicts me so, as doth his poisoned view:<br>That is most false, should most of all be true. Exit |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Now, lords, our fleeting barque is under sail,<br>Our gage is thrown, and war is soon begun,<br>But not so quickly brought unto an end. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir William Montague |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | But wherefore comes Sir William Montague?<br>(To Montague) How stands the league between the Scot and us? |
| MONTAGUE | Cracked and dissevered, my renowned lord.<br>The treacherous [[archetypes/king\|King]] no sooner was informed<br>Of your withdrawing of your army back<br>But straight, forgetting of his former oath,<br>He made invasion on the bordering towns.<br>Berwick is won, Newcastle spoiled and lost,<br>And now the [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]] hath begirt with siege<br>The [[settings/castle\|Castle]] of Roxburgh, where, enclosed,<br>The Countess Salisbury is like to perish. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (to Warwick)<br>That is thy daughter, Warwick, is it not?<br>Whose husband hath in Bretagne served so long<br>About the planting of Lord Montfort there? |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | It is, my lord. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Ignoble David, hast thou none to grieve<br>But seely ladies with thy threat’ning arms?<br>But I will make you shrink your snaily horns.<br>(To Audley) First, therefore, Audley, this shall be thy charge:<br>Go levy footmen for our wars in France.<br>(To the Prince of Wales) And, Ned, take muster of our men-at-arms.<br>In every shire elect a several band.<br>Let them be soldiers of a lusty spirit,<br>Such as dread nothing but dishonour’s blot.<br>Be wary therefore, since we do commence<br>A famous war, and with so mighty a nation.<br>(To Derby) Derby, be thou ambassador for us<br>Unto our father-in-law, the Earl of Hainault.<br>Make him acquainted with our enterprise,<br>And likewise will him, with our own allies<br>That are in Flanders, to solicit, too,<br>The Emperor of Almagne in our name.<br>Myself, whilst you are jointly thus employed,<br>Will, with these forces that I have at hand,<br>March and once more repulse the traitorous Scot.<br>But sirs, be resolute. We shall have wars<br>On every side. (To the Prince of Wales) And, Ned, thou must begin<br>Now to forget thy study and thy books,<br>And ure thy shoulders to an armour’s weight. |
| PRINCE OF WALES | As cheerful sounding to my youthful spleen<br>This tumult is of war’s increasing broils,<br>As at the coronation of a [[archetypes/king\|king]]<br>The joyful clamours of the people are<br>When ‘Ave Caesar’ they pronounce aloud. |
| *(didascalia)* | Within this school of [[concepts/honour\|honour]] I shall learn |
| PRINCE OF WALES | Either to sacrifice my foes to [[motifs/death\|death]],<br>Or, in a rightful quarrel, spend my breath.<br>Then cheerfully forward, each a several way.<br>In great affairs ’tis naught to use delay. Exeunt<br><br>Sc. 2 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|Countess of Salisbury]], above |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Alas, how much in vain my poor eyes gaze<br>For succour that my sovereign should send.<br>Ah, cousin Montague, I [[concepts/fear\|fear]] thou wants<br>The lively spirit sharply to solicit<br>With vehement suit the [[archetypes/king\|King]] in my behalf.<br>Thou dost not tell him what a grief it is<br>To be the scornful captive to a Scot,<br>Either to be wooed with broad untuned oaths,<br>Or forced by rough insulting barbarism.<br>Thou dost not tell him, if he here prevail,<br>How much they will deride us in the North,<br>And in their vile, uncivil, skipping jigs<br>Bray forth their conquest and our overthrow<br>Even in the barren, bleak and fruitless air— |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter below David [[archetypes/king\|King]] of Scotland and Sir William Douglas with soldiers, meeting the Due de Lorraine |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | (Aside) I must withdraw. The everlasting foe<br>Comes to the wall. I’ll closely step aside<br>And list their babble, blunt and full of [[concepts/pride\|pride]].<br>The Countess withdraws |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF SCOTLAND | My lord of Lorraine, to our brother of France<br>Commend us as the man in Christendom<br>That we most reverence and entirely [[motifs/love\|love]].<br>Touching your embassage, return and say<br>That we with England will not enter parley,<br>Nor never make fair weather, or take truce,<br>But burn their neighbour towns, and so persist<br>With eager roads beyond their [[motifs/city\|city]] York;<br>And never shall our bonny riders rest,<br>Nor rusting canker have the [[motifs/time\|time]] to eat<br>Their light-borne snaffle, nor their nimble spur,<br>Nor lay aside their jacks of gimmaled mail,<br>Nor hang their staves of grained Scottish ash<br>In peaceful wise upon their [[motifs/city\|city]] walls,<br>Nor from their buttoned tawny leathern belts<br>Dismiss their biting whinyards, till your [[archetypes/king\|King]]<br>Cry out, ‘Enough! Spare England now for pity!’<br>Farewell, and tell him that you leave us here,<br>Before this [[settings/castle\|castle]]; say you came from us<br>Even when we had that yielded to our hands. |
| DUC DE LORRAINE | Take I my leave, and fairly will return<br>Your acceptable greeting to my [[archetypes/king\|King]]. Exit |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF SCOTLAND | (to Douglas)<br>Now, Douglas, to our former task again<br>For the division of this certain spoil. |
| DOUGLAS | My liege, I crave the lady, and no more. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF SCOTLAND | Nay, soft ye, sir; first I must make my choice,<br>And first I do bespeak her for myself. |
| DOUGLAS | Why then, my liege, let me enjoy her jewels. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF SCOTLAND | Those are her own, still liable to her;<br>And who inherits her hath those with all. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Scottish messenger in haste |
| MESSENGER | My liege, as we were pricking on the hills<br>To fetch in booty, marching hitherward<br>We might descry a mighty host of men.<br>The sun, reflecting on the armour, showed<br>A field of plate; a wood of picks advanced.<br>Bethink your highness speedily herein:<br>An easy march within four hours will bring<br>The hindmost rank unto this place, my liege. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF SCOTLAND | Dislodge! Dislodge! It is the [[archetypes/king\|King]] of England! |
| *(didascalia)* | DOUGLAS to the Messenger |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF SCOTLAND | Jemmy, my man, saddle my bonny black. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF SCOTLAND | Mean’st thou to fight, Douglas? We are too weak. |
| DOUGLAS | I know it [[motifs/well\|well]], my liege, and therefore fly. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | (coming forward above)<br>My lords of Scotland, will ye stay and drink? |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF SCOTLAND | She mocks us, Douglas. I cannot endure it. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Say, good my lord, which is he must have the lady,<br>And which her jewels? I am sure, my lords,<br>Ye will not hence till you have shared the spoils. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF SCOTLAND | She heard the messenger and heard our talk,<br>And now that comfort makes her scorn at us. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter another Scottish messenger |
| SECOND MESSENGER | Arm, my good lord! O we are all surprised!<br>After the French ambassador, my liege,<br>And tell him that you dare not ride to York. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] (to the [[archetypes/king\|King]] of Scotland) |
| SECOND MESSENGER | Excuse it that your bonny [[motifs/horse\|horse]] is lame. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF SCOTLAND aside |
| SECOND MESSENGER | She heard that too! Intolerable grief!<br>(To the Countess) Woman, farewell, although I do not stay— |
| *(didascalia)* | ‘Tis not for [[concepts/fear\|fear]], and yet you run away. Exeunt Scots |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | O happy comfort, welcome to our house!<br>The confident and boist’rous boasting Scot,<br>That swore before my walls they would not back<br>For all the armed power of this land,<br>With faceless [[concepts/fear\|fear]] that ever turns his back,<br>Turned hence against the blasting north-east [[motifs/wind\|wind]]<br>Upon the bare report and name of arms! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir William de Montague with soldiers |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | O [[motifs/summer\|summer]]’s day! See where my cousin comes! |
| MONTAGUE | How fares my aunt? We are not Scots—<br>Why do you shut your gates against your friends? |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]] may I give a welcome, coz, to thee,<br>For thou com’st [[motifs/well\|well]] to chase my foes from hence. |
| MONTAGUE | The [[archetypes/king\|King]] himself is come in person hither.<br>Dear aunt, descend, and gratulate his highness. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | How may I entertain his majesty<br>To show my duty and his dignity? |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit from above |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[archetypes/king\|King]] Edward, the [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|Earl of Warwick]] and the Comte d’Artois, with others, including Lodowick |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | What, are the stealing foxes fled and gone<br>Before we could uncouple at their heels? |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | They are, my liege, but with a cheerful cry<br>Hot hounds and hardy chase them at the heels. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|Countess of Salisbury]] below |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | This is the Countess, Warwick, is it not? |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | Even she, my liege, whose [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] tyrants’ [[concepts/fear\|fear]]—<br>As a May blossom with pernicious winds—<br>Hath sullied, withered, overcast and done. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Hath she been fairer, Warwick, than she is? |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | My gracious [[archetypes/king\|King]], fair is she not at all<br>If that her [[concepts/self\|self]] were by to stain herself<br>As I have seen her when she was her [[concepts/self\|self]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD aside |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | What strange enchantment lurked in those her eyes,<br>When they excelled this excellence they have,<br>That now her dim decline hath power to draw<br>My subject eyes from piercing majesty<br>To gaze on her with doting admiration? |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | (kneeling before [[archetypes/king\|King]] Edward)<br>In duty, lower than the ground I kneel,<br>And fore my dull knees bow my feeling heart,<br>To witness my obedience to your highness<br>With many millions of a subject’s thanks<br>For this your royal presence, whose approach<br>Hath driven war and danger from my gate. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Lady, stand up. I come to bring thee peace,<br>However thereby I have purchased war. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | (rising)<br>No war to you, my liege. The Scots are gone<br>And gallop [[motifs/home\|home]] toward Scotland with their hate. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (aside)<br>Lest yielding here I pine in shameful [[motifs/love\|love]]—<br>(Aloud) Come, we’ll pursue the Scots. Artois, away! |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | A little while, my gracious sovereign, stay,<br>And let the power of a mighty [[archetypes/king\|king]]<br>[[concepts/honour\|Honour]] our roof. My husband, in the wars,<br>When he shall hear it, will triumph for joy.<br>Then, dear my liege, now niggard not thy state;<br>Being at the wall, enter our homely gate. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Pardon me, Countess, I will come no near:<br>I dreamed tonight of treason, and I [[concepts/fear\|fear]]. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Far from this place let ugly treason lie. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (aside)<br>No farther off than her conspiring eye,<br>Which shoots infected [[motifs/poison\|poison]] in my heart<br>Beyond repulse of wit or cure of art.<br>Now in the sun alone it doth not lie<br>With light to take light from a mortal eye;<br>For here two day-[[motifs/stars\|stars]] that mine eyes would see<br>More than the sun steals mine own light from me.<br>Contemplative [[motifs/desire\|desire]], [[motifs/desire\|desire]] to be<br>In contemplation that may master thee.<br>(Aloud) Warwick, Artois, to [[motifs/horse\|horse]] and let’s away! |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | What might I speak to make my sovereign stay? |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (aside)<br>What needs a tongue to such a speaking eye<br>That more persuades than winning oratory? |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Let not thy presence, like the April sun,<br>Flatter our earth and suddenly be done.<br>More happy do not make our outward wall<br>Than thou wilt grace our inner house withal.<br>Our house, my liege, is like a country swain,<br>Whose habit rude, and manners blunt and plain,<br>Presageth naught, yet inly beautified<br>With bounty’s riches and fair hidden [[concepts/pride\|pride]].<br>For where the golden ore doth buried lie,<br>The ground, undecked with [[concepts/nature\|nature]]’s tapestry,<br>Seems barren, sere, unfertile, fruitless, dry;<br>And where the upper turf of earth doth boast<br>His [[concepts/pride\|pride]], perfumes and parti-coloured cost,<br>Delve there and find this issue and their [[concepts/pride\|pride]]<br>To [[motifs/spring\|spring]] from ordure and corruptious stied.<br>But, to make up my all-too-long compare,<br>These ragged walls no testimony are<br>What is within, but like a cloak doth hide<br>From weather’s waste the under garnished [[concepts/pride\|pride]].<br>More gracious than my terms can, let thee be:<br>Entreat thyself to stay a while with me. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | As wise as fair—what fond fit can be heard<br>When wisdom keeps the gate as [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]]’s guard?<br>Countess, albeit my business urgeth me<br>It shall attend while I attend on thee.<br>Come on, my lords, here will I host tonight. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but Lodowick |
| LODOWICK | I might perceive his eye in her eye lost,<br>His ear to drink her sweet tongue’s utterance,<br>And changing passion, like inconstant clouds<br>That rack upon the carriage of the winds,<br>Increase and die in his disturbed cheeks.<br>Lo, when she blushed, even then did he look pale,<br>As if her cheeks by some enchanted power<br>Attracted had the cherry [[motifs/blood\|blood]] from his.<br>Anon, with reverent [[concepts/fear\|fear]] when she grew pale,<br>His cheeks put on their scarlet ornaments,<br>But no more like her oriental red<br>Than brick to coral or live things to dead.<br>Why did he then thus counterfeit her looks?<br>If she did blush, ‘twas tender modest shame<br>Being in the sacred presence of a [[archetypes/king\|king]].<br>If he did blush, ’twas rude immodest shame<br>To vail his eyes amiss, being a [[archetypes/king\|king]].<br>If she looked pale, ‘twas seely woman’s [[concepts/fear\|fear]]<br>To bear herself in presence of a [[archetypes/king\|king]].<br>If he looked pale, it was with guilty [[concepts/fear\|fear]]<br>To dote amiss, being a mighty [[archetypes/king\|king]].<br>Then, Scottish wars, farewell. I [[concepts/fear\|fear]] ’twill prove<br>A ling’ring English siege of peevish [[motifs/love\|love]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[archetypes/king\|King]] Edward |
| LODOWICK | Here comes his highness, walking all alone. |
| *(didascalia)* | Lodowick withdraws |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (aside)<br>She is grown more fairer far since I came hither,<br>Her voice more silver every word than other,<br>Her wit more fluent. What a strange discourse<br>Unfolded she of David and his Scots:<br>‘Even thus’, quoth she, ‘he spake,’ and then spoke broad,<br>With epithets and accents of the Scot,<br>But somewhat better than the Scot could speak.<br>‘And thus,’ quoth she, and answered then herself,<br>For who could speak like her? But she herself<br>Breathes from the wall an angel’s note from heaven<br>Of sweet defiance to her barbarous foes.<br>When she would talk of peace, methinks her tongue<br>Commanded war to [[motifs/prison\|prison]]; when of war,<br>It wakened Caesar from his Roman [[motifs/grave\|grave]]<br>To hear war beautified by her discourse;<br>Wisdom is foolishness but in her tongue,<br>[[concepts/beauty\|Beauty]] a slander but in her fair face,<br>There is no [[motifs/summer\|summer]] but in her cheerful looks,<br>Nor frosty [[motifs/winter\|winter]] but in her disdain.<br>I cannot blame the Scots that did besiege her,<br>For she is all the treasure of our land,<br>But call them cowards that they ran away,<br>Having so rich and fair a cause to stay.<br>(Aloud) Art thou there, Lod’wick? |
| *(didascalia)* | Lodowick comes forward |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Give me ink and paper. |
| LODOWICK | I will, my liege. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | And bid the lords hold on their play at chess,<br>For we will walk and meditate alone. |
| LODOWICK | I will, my sovereign. Exit |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | This fellow is [[motifs/well\|well]] read in poetry,<br>And hath a lusty and persuasive spirit.<br>I will acquaint him with my passion,<br>Which he shall [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]] with a veil of lawn,<br>Through which the [[characters/queen\|queen]] of [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]]’s [[characters/queen\|queen]] shall see<br>Herself the ground of my infirmity. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lodowick, with pen, ink and paper |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Hast thou pen, ink and paper ready, Lod’wick? |
| LODOWICK | Ready, my liege. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Then in the [[motifs/summer\|summer]] arbour sit by me;<br>Make it our council house or cabinet:<br>Since green our thoughts, green be the conventicle<br>Where we will ease us by disburd’ning them. |
| *(didascalia)* | They sit. Lodowick prepares to write |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Now, Lod‘wick, invocate some golden muse<br>To bring thee hither an enchanted pen<br>That may for sighs set down true sighs indeed,<br>Talking of grief, to make thee ready groan,<br>And when thou write’st of [[motifs/tears\|tears]], encouch the word<br>Before and after with such sweet laments<br>That it may raise drops in a Tartar’s eye,<br>And make a flint-heart Scythian pitiful—<br>For so much moving hath a poet’s pen.<br>Then, if thou be a poet, move thou so<br>And be enriched by thy sovereign’s [[motifs/love\|love]].<br>For if the touch of sweet concordant strings<br>Could force attendance in the ears of hell,<br>How much more shall the strains of poets’ wit<br>Beguile and ravish soft and human minds? |
| LODOWICK | To whom, my lord, shall I direct my style? |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | To one that stains the fair and sots the wise,<br>Whose [[concepts/body\|body]] is an abstract or a brief,<br>Contains each general virtue in the world.<br>‘Better than beautiful’, thou must begin,<br>Devise for fair a fairer word than ‘fair’,<br>And every ornament that thou wouldst praise,<br>Fly it a pitch above the soar of praise.<br>For flattery [[concepts/fear\|fear]] thou not to be convicted,<br>For were thy admiration ten times more,<br>Ten times ten thousand more the worth exceeds<br>Of that thou art to praise thy praise’s worth.<br>Begin; I will to contemplate the while.<br>Forget not to set down how passionate,<br>How heart-sick and how full of languishment<br>Her [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] makes me. |
| LODOWICK | Write I to a woman? |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | What [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] else could triumph over me,<br>Or who but women do our [[motifs/love\|love]]-lays greet?<br>What think’st thou I did bid thee praise? A [[motifs/horse\|horse]]? |
| LODOWICK | Of what condition or estate she is<br>’Twere requisite that I should know, my lord. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Of such estate that hers is as a throne,<br>And my estate the footstool where she treads.<br>Then mayst thou judge what her condition is<br>By the proportion of her mightiness.<br>Write on, while I peruse her in my thoughts. |
| *(didascalia)* |  |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Her voice to music or the [[motifs/nightingale\|nightingale]].<br>To music every [[motifs/summer\|summer]]-leaping swain<br>Compares his sunburnt [[archetypes/lover\|lover]] when she speaks,<br>And why should I speak of the [[motifs/nightingale\|nightingale]]?<br>The [[motifs/nightingale\|nightingale]] sings of adulterate wrong<br>And that compare is too satirical,<br>For sin, though sin, would not be so esteemed,<br>But rather virtue sin, sin virtue deemed.<br>Her hair far softer than the silkworm’s twist,<br>Like to a flattering glass doth make more fair<br>The yellow amber—‘like a flattering glass’<br>Comes in too soon: for writing of her eyes<br>I’ll say that like a glass they catch the sun,<br>And thence the hot reflection doth rebound<br>Against my breast and burns my heart within.<br>Ah, what a world of descant makes my [[motifs/soul\|soul]]<br>Upon this voluntary ground of [[motifs/love\|love]]!<br>Come, Lod’wick: hast thou turned thy ink to gold?<br>If not, write but in letters capital<br>My mistress’ name, and it will gild thy paper.<br>Read, Lod’wick, read!<br>Fill thou the empty hollows of mine ears<br>With the sweet hearing of thy poetry! |
| LODOWICK | I have not to a period brought her praise. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Her praise is as my [[motifs/love\|Love]]—both infinite,<br>Which apprehend such violent extremes<br>That they disdain an ending period.<br>Her [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] hath no match but my affection;<br>Hers more than most, mine most, and more than more;<br>Hers more to praise than tell the sea by drops—<br>Nay more!—than drop the massy earth by sands,<br>And sand by sand print them in [[motifs/memory\|memory]].<br>Then wherefore talk’st thou of a period<br>To that which craves unended admiration?<br>Read. Let us hear. |
| LODOWICK | (reading)<br>‘More fair and chaste than is the [[characters/queen\|queen]] of shades’— |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (staying him)<br>That line hath two faults, gross and palpable.<br>Compar’st thou her to the pale [[characters/queen\|queen]] of [[motifs/night\|night]],<br>Who, being set in dark, seems therefore light?<br>What is she when the sun lifts up his head<br>But like a fading taper, dim and dead?<br>My [[motifs/love\|love]] shall brave the eye of heaven at [[motifs/noon\|noon]],<br>And, being unmasked, outshine the golden sun! |
| LODOWICK | What is the other fault, my sovereign lord? |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Read o’er the line again. |
| LODOWICK | (reading) ‘More fair and chaste’— |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (staying him)<br>I did not bid thee talk of chastity,<br>To ransack so the treasure of her mind,<br>For I had rather have her chased than chaste!<br>Out with the [[motifs/moon\|moon]] line! I will none of it.<br>And let me have her likened to the sun-<br>Say she hath thrice more splendour than the sun,<br>That her perfections emulates the sun,<br>That she breeds sweets as plenteous as the sun,<br>That she doth thaw cold [[motifs/winter\|winter]] like the sun,<br>That she doth cheer fresh [[motifs/summer\|summer]] like the sun,<br>That she doth dazzle gazers like the sun,<br>And in this application to the sun<br>Bid her be free and general as the sun,<br>Who smiles upon the basest weed that grows<br>As lovingly as on the fragrant [[motifs/rose\|rose]].<br>Let’s see what follows that same moonlight line. |
| LODOWICK | (reading)<br>‘More fair and chaste than is the [[characters/queen\|queen]] of shades,<br>More bold in constancy’— |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (staying him)<br>In constancy than who? |
| LODOWICK | (reading)<br>‘...than Judith was.’ |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | O monstrous line! Put in the next a sword<br>And I shall woo her to cut off my head!<br>Blot, blot, good Lod’wick. Let us hear the next. |
| LODOWICK | There’s all that yet is done. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | I thank thee then. Thou hast done little ill,<br>But what is done is passing passing ill.<br>No, let the captain talk of boist’rous war,<br>The prisoner of emurèd dark constraint;<br>The sick man best sets down the pangs of [[motifs/death\|death]],<br>The man that starves the sweetness of a feast,<br>The frozen [[motifs/soul\|soul]] the benefit of [[motifs/fire\|fire]],<br>And every grief his happy opposite.<br>[[motifs/love\|Love]] cannot sound [[motifs/well\|well]] but in lovers’ tongues.<br>Give me the pen and paper. I will write.<br>Lodowick gives him the pen and paper. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|Countess of Salisbury]] |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (Aside) But soft—here comes the treasurer of my spirit.<br>(Aloud to Lodowick, showing him the paper in his hand)<br>Lod‘wick, thou know’st not how to draw a battle!<br>These wings, these flankers and these squadrons<br>Argue in thee defective discipline.<br>Thou shouldst have placed this here, this other here— |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Pardon my boldness, my thrice-gracious lords.<br>Let my intrusion here be called my duty<br>That comes to see my sovereign how he fares. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (to Lodowick, giving him the paper)<br>Go, draw the same, I tell thee in what form. |
| LODOWICK | I go. Exit |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Sorry I am to see my liege so sad.<br>What may thy subject do to drive from thee<br>Thy gloomy consort, sullen [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]]? |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Ah, lady, I am blunt and cannot strew<br>The [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]] of solace in a ground of shame.<br>Since I came hither, Countess, I am wronged. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Now God forbid that any in my house<br>Should think my sovereign wrong! Thrice-gentle [[archetypes/king\|King]],<br>Acquaint me with thy cause of discontent. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | How near, then, shall I be to remedy? |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | As near, my liege, as all my woman’s power<br>Can pawn itself to buy thy remedy. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | If thou speak’st true, then have I my redress.<br>Engage thy power to redeem my joys,<br>And I am joyful, Countess; else I die. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | I will, my liege. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Swear, Countess, that thou wilt. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | By heaven, I will. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Then take thyself a little way aside<br>And tell thyself a [[archetypes/king\|king]] doth dote on thee.<br>Say that within thy power it doth lie<br>To make him happy, and that thou hast sworn<br>To give him all the joy within thy power-<br>Do this, and tell me when I shall be happy. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | All this is done, my thrice-dread sovereign.<br>That power of [[motifs/love\|love]] that I have power to give<br>Thou hast, with all devout obedience.<br>Employ me how thou wilt in proof thereof. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Thou hear’st me say that I do dote on thee. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | If on my [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]], take it if thou canst;<br>Though little, I do prize it ten times less.<br>If on my virtue, take it if thou canst;<br>For virtue’s store, by giving, doth augment.<br>Be it on what it will that I can give,<br>And thou canst take away, inherit it. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | It is thy [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] that I would enjoy. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | O, were it painted I would wipe it off<br>And dispossess myself to give it thee!<br>But, sovereign, it is soldered to my life:<br>Take one, and both, for, like an humble [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]],<br>It haunts the sunshine of my [[motifs/summer\|summer]]’s life— |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | But thou mayst lend it me to sport withal. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | As easy may my intellectual [[motifs/soul\|soul]]<br>Be lent away and yet my [[concepts/body\|body]] live<br>As lend my [[concepts/body\|body]], palace to my [[motifs/soul\|soul]],<br>Away from her and yet retain my [[motifs/soul\|soul]].<br>My [[concepts/body\|body]] is her bower, her [[settings/court\|court]], her abbey;<br>And she an angel, pure, divine, unspotted.<br>If I should lend her house, my lord, to thee,<br>I kill my poor [[motifs/soul\|soul]], and my poor [[motifs/soul\|soul]] me. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Didst thou not swear to give me what I would? |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | I did, my liege, so what you would I could. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | I wish no more of thee than thou mayst give,<br>Nor beg I do not, but I rather buy—<br>That is, thy [[motifs/love\|love]]; and for that [[motifs/love\|love]] of thine<br>In rich exchange I tender to thee mine. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | But that your lips were sacred, good my lord,<br>You would profane the holy name of [[motifs/love\|love]].<br>That [[motifs/love\|love]] you offer me you cannot give,<br>For Caesar owes that tribute to his [[characters/queen\|queen]].<br>That [[motifs/love\|love]] you beg of me I cannot give,<br>For Sarah owes that duty to her lord.<br>He that doth clip or counterfeit your stamp<br>Shall die, my lord: and will your sacred [[concepts/self\|self]]<br>Commit high treason ‘gainst the [[archetypes/king\|king]] of heaven<br>To stamp his image in forbidden metal,<br>Forgetting your allegiance and your oath?<br>In violating [[motifs/marriage\|marriage]]’ sacred law<br>You break a greater [[concepts/honour\|honour]] than yourself:<br>To be a [[archetypes/king\|king]] is of a younger house<br>Than to be married. Your progenitor,<br>Sole reigning Adam o’er the universe,<br>By God was honoured for a married man,<br>But not by him anointed for a [[archetypes/king\|king]].<br>It is a penalty to break your statutes,<br>Though not enacted with your highness’ hand;<br>How much more to infringe the holy act<br>Made by the mouth of God, sealed with his hand!<br>I know my sovereign—in my husband’s [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>Who now doth loyal service in his wars—<br>Doth but so try the wife of Salisbury,<br>Whether she will hear a wanton’s tale or no.<br>Lest being therein guilty by my stay,<br>From that, not from my liege, I turn away. Exit |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Whether is her [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] by her words divine,<br>Or are her words sweet chaplains to her [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]]?<br>Like as the [[motifs/wind\|wind]] doth beautify a sail<br>And as a sail becomes the unseen [[motifs/wind\|wind]],<br>So do her words her beauties, [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] words.<br>O, that I were a honey-gathering bee<br>To bear the comb of virtue from this flower,<br>And not a [[motifs/poison\|poison]]-sucking, envious spider<br>To turn the juice I take to deadly venoml<br>Religion is austere, and [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] gentle—<br>Too strict a guardian for so fair a ward.<br>O, that she were as is the air to me!<br>Why, so she is: for when I would embrace her,<br>This do I (embracing the air), and catch nothing but myself.<br>I must enjoy her, for I cannot beat<br>With reason and reproof fond [[motifs/love\|love]] away. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|Earl of Warwick]] |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (Aside) Here comes her father. I will work with him<br>To bear my colours in this field of [[motifs/love\|love]]. |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | How is it that my sovereign is so sad?<br>May I, with pardon, know your highness’ grief?<br>An that my old endeavour will remove it,<br>It shall not cumber long your majesty. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | A kind and voluntary gift thou proffer’st<br>That I was forward to have begged of thee.<br>But, O, thou world, great [[characters/nurse\|nurse]] of flattery,<br>Why dost thou tip men’s tongues with golden words,<br>And peise their deeds with weight of heavy lead<br>That fair performance cannot follow promise?<br>O, that a man might hold the heart’s close book<br>And choke the lavish tongue when it doth utter<br>The breath of falsehood not charactered there! |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | Far be it from the [[concepts/honour\|honour]] of my age<br>That I should owe bright gold and render lead.<br>Age is a cynic, not a flatterer.<br>I say again that if I knew your grief,<br>And that by me it may be lessened,<br>My proper harm should buy your highness’ good. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | These are the vulgar tenders of false men<br>That never pay the duty of their words.<br>Thou wilt not stick to swear what thou hast said,<br>But when thou know’st my griefs condition<br>This rash disgorged vomit of thy word<br>Thou wilt eat up again, and leave me helpless. |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | By heaven, I will not, though your majesty<br>Did bid me run upon your sword and die! |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Say that my grief is no way medicinable<br>But by the loss and bruising of thine [[concepts/honour\|honour]]? |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | If nothing but that loss may vantage you<br>I would account that loss my vantage too. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Think’st that thou canst unswear thy oath again? |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | I cannot, nor I would not if I could. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | But if thou dost, what shall I say to thee? |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | What may be said to any perjured [[archetypes/villain\|villain]]<br>That breaks the sacred warrant of an oath. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | What wilt thou say to one that breaks an oath? |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | That he hath broke his [[concepts/faith\|faith]] with God and man,<br>And from them both stands excommunicate. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | What office were it to suggest a man<br>To break a lawful and religious vow? |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | An office for the devil, not for man. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | That devil’s office must thou do for me,<br>Or break thy oath and cancel all the bonds<br>Of [[motifs/love\|love]] and duty ‘twixt thyself and me.<br>And therefore, Warwick, if thou art thyself,<br>The lord and master of thy word and oath,<br>Go to thy daughter and, in my behalf,<br>Command her, woo her, win her any ways<br>To be my mistress and my secret [[motifs/love\|love]].<br>I will not stand to hear thee make reply;<br>Thy oath break hers, or let thy sovereign die. Exit |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | O doting [[archetypes/king\|king]]! O detestable office!<br>[[motifs/well\|Well]] may I tempt myself to wrong myself,<br>When he hath sworn me by the name of God<br>To break a vow made by the name of God.<br>What if I swear by this right hand of mine<br>To cut this right hand off? The better way<br>Were to profane the idol than confound it,<br>But neither will I do. I’ll keep mine oath<br>And to my daughter make a recantation<br>Of all the virtue I have preached to her.<br>I’ll say she must forget her husband, Salisbury—<br>If she remember to embrace the [[archetypes/king\|King]].<br>I’ll say an oath may easily be broken—<br>But not so easily pardoned, being broken.<br>I’ll say it is true [[concepts/charity\|charity]] to [[motifs/love\|love]]—<br>But not true [[motifs/love\|love]] to be so charitable.<br>I’ll say his [[concepts/greatness\|greatness]] may bear out the shame—<br>But not his kingdom can buy out the sin.<br>I’ll say it is my duty to persuade—<br>But not her honesty to give consent. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|Countess of Salisbury]] |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | (Aside) See where she comes. Was never father had<br>Against his [[archetypes/child\|child]] an embassage so bad. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | My lord and father, I have sought for you.<br>My mother and the peers importune you<br>To keep in presence of his majesty,<br>And do your best to make his highness merry. |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | (aside)<br>How shall I enter in this graceless errand?<br>I must not call her [[archetypes/child\|child]], for where’s the father<br>That will in such a suit seduce his [[archetypes/child\|child]]?<br>Then ‘wife of Salisbury’—shall I so begin?<br>No, he’s my friend, and where is found the friend<br>That will do [[concepts/friendship\|friendship]] such endamagement?<br>(To the Countess) Neither my daughter, nor my dear friend’s wife,<br>I am not Warwick, as thou think‘st I am,<br>But an attorney from the [[settings/court\|court]] of hell,<br>That thus have housed my spirit in his form<br>To do a message to thee from the [[archetypes/king\|King]]:<br>‘The mighty [[archetypes/king\|King]] of England dotes on thee:<br>He that hath power to take away thy life<br>Hath power to take thy [[concepts/honour\|honour]]. Then consent<br>To pawn thine [[concepts/honour\|honour]] rather than thy life;<br>[[concepts/honour\|Honour]] is often lost and got again,<br>But life, once gone, hath no recovery.<br>The sun that withers hay doth nourish grass,<br>The [[archetypes/king\|King]] that would distain thee, will advance thee.<br>The poets write that great [[characters/achilles\|Achilles]]’ spear<br>Could heal the wound it made; the moral is,<br>What mighty men misdo they can amend.<br>The [[motifs/lion\|lion]] doth become his bloody jaws<br>And grace his foragement by being mild<br>When vassal [[concepts/fear\|fear]] lies trembling at his feet.<br>The [[archetypes/king\|King]] will, in his glory, hide thy shame,<br>And those that gaze on him, to find out thee,<br>Will lose their eyesight looking in the sun.<br>What can one drop of [[motifs/poison\|poison]] harm the sea<br>Whose hugy vastures can digest the ill<br>And make it lose his operation?<br>The [[archetypes/king\|King]]’s great name will temper thy misdeeds,<br>And give the bitter potion of reproach<br>A sugared, sweet and most delicious taste.<br>Besides, it is no harm to do the thing<br>Which, without shame, could not be left undone.’<br>Thus have I, in his majesty’s behalf,<br>Apparelled sin in virtuous sentences,<br>And dwell upon thy answer in his suit. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Unnatural besiege! Woe me unhappy,<br>To have escaped the danger of my foes<br>And to be ten times worse envir‘ned by friends!<br>Hath he no means to stain my honest [[motifs/blood\|blood]]<br>But to corrupt the author of my [[motifs/blood\|blood]]<br>To be his scandalous and vile solicitor?<br>No marvel though the branch be then infected,<br>When [[motifs/poison\|poison]] hath encompassed the root;<br>No marvel though the leprous infant die,<br>When the stern dame envenometh the dug.<br>Why then, give sin a passport to offend,<br>And youth the dangerous rein of liberty.<br>Blot out the strict forbidding of the law,<br>And cancel every canon that prescribes<br>A shame for shame, or penance for offence.<br>No, let me die if his too boist’rous will<br>Will have it so, before I will consent<br>To be an actor in his graceless lust. |
| [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|EARL OF WARWICK]] | Why, now thou speak‘st as I would have thee speak!<br>And mark how I unsay my words again:<br>An honourable [[motifs/grave\|grave]] is more esteemed<br>Than the polluted closet of a [[archetypes/king\|king]].<br>The greater man, the greater is the thing,<br>Be it good or bad, that he shall undertake.<br>An unreputed mote flying in the sun<br>Presents a greater substance than it is.<br>The freshest [[motifs/summer\|summer]]’s day doth soonest taint<br>The loathed carrion that it seems to kiss.<br>Deep are the blows made with a mighty axe.<br>That sin doth ten times aggravate itself<br>That is committed in a holy place.<br>An evil deed done by authority<br>Is sin and subornation. Deck an ape<br>In tissue, and the [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] of the robe<br>Adds but the greater scorn unto the beast.<br>A spacious field of reasons could I urge<br>Between his glory, daughter, and thy shame:<br>That [[motifs/poison\|poison]] shows worst in a golden cup;<br>Dark [[motifs/night\|night]] seems darker by the [[motifs/lightning\|lightning]] flash;<br>Lilies that fester smell far worse than weeds;<br>And every glory that inclines to sin,<br>The shame is treble by the opposite.<br>So leave I with my blessing in thy bosom,<br>Which then convert to a most heavy curse<br>When thou convert’st from [[concepts/honour\|honour]]’s golden name<br>To the black faction of bed-blotting shame. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | I’ll follow thee, and when my mind turns so,<br>My [[concepts/body\|body]] sink my [[motifs/soul\|soul]] in endless woe. Exeunt<br><br>Sc. 3 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter at one door the Earl of Derby from France. At another door, enter Lord Audley with a drummer |
| EARL OF DERBY | Thrice-noble Audley, [[motifs/well\|well]] encountered here.<br>How is it with our sovereign and his peers? |
| AUDLEY | ‘Tis full a fortnight since I saw his highness,<br>What [[motifs/time\|time]] he sent me forth to muster men,<br>Which I accordingly have done, and bring them hither,<br>In fair array, before his majesty.<br>What news, my lord of Derby, from the Emperor? |
| EARL OF DERBY | As good as we [[motifs/desire\|desire]]. The Emperor<br>Hath yielded to his highness friendly aid,<br>And makes our [[archetypes/king\|king]] lieutenant-general<br>In all his lands and large dominions.<br>Then via for the spacious bounds of France! |
| AUDLEY | What, doth his highness leap to hear these news? |
| EARL OF DERBY | I have not yet found [[motifs/time\|time]] to open them.<br>The [[archetypes/king\|King]] is in his closet, malcontent.<br>For what I know not, but he gave in charge<br>Till after dinner none should interrupt him.<br>The Countess Salisbury and her father Warwick,<br>Artois, and all, look underneath the brows. |
| AUDLEY | Undoubtedly, then, something is amiss. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound trumpets within |
| EARL OF DERBY | The trumpets sound. The [[archetypes/king\|King]] is now abroad. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[archetypes/king\|King]] Edward |
| COMTE D’ARTOIS | Here comes his highness. |
| EARL OF DERBY | (to the [[archetypes/king\|King]])<br>Befall my sovereign all my sovereign’s wish. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD aside |
| EARL OF DERBY | Ah, that thou wert a [[characters/witch\|witch]] to make it so. |
| EARL OF DERBY | The Emperor greeteth you— |
| *(didascalia)* | [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD aside Would it were the Countess. |
| EARL OF DERBY | —And hath accorded to your highness’ suit. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD aside |
| EARL OF DERBY | Thou liest. She hath not, but I would she had. |
| AUDLEY | All [[motifs/love\|love]] and duty to my lord the [[archetypes/king\|King]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD aside |
| AUDLEY | [[motifs/well\|Well]], all but one is none. (To Audley) What news with you? |
| AUDLEY | I have, my liege, levied those [[motifs/horse\|horse]] and foot,<br>According as your charge, and brought them hither. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Then let those foot trudge hence upon those [[motifs/horse\|horse]],<br>According to our discharge, and be gone.<br>Derby, I’ll look upon the Countess’ mind anon. |
| EARL OF DERBY | The Countess’ mind, my liege? |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | I mean the Emperor. Leave me alone. |
| AUDLEY | (to Derby)<br>What is his mind? |
| EARL OF DERBY | Let’s leave him to his humour. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Derby and Audley |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Thus from the heart’s abundance speaks the tongue:<br>‘Countess’ for ‘Emperor’—and indeed why not?<br>She is as imperator over me, and I to her<br>Am as a kneeling vassal that observes<br>The pleasure or displeasure of her eye. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lodowick |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (To Lodowick) What says the more-than-[[characters/cleopatra\|Cleopatra]]’s<br>match<br>To Caesar now? |
| LODOWICK | That yet, my liege, ere [[motifs/night\|night]]<br>She will resolve your majesty. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound drum within |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | What drum is this that thunders forth this march<br>To start the tender Cupid in my bosom?<br>Poor sheepskin, how it brawls with him that beateth it!<br>Go, break the thund’ring parchment-[[characters/bottom\|bottom]] out<br>And I will teach it to conduct sweet lines<br>Unto the bosom of a heavenly nymph;<br>For I will use it as my writing paper,<br>And so reduce him from a scolding drum<br>To be the herald, and dear counsel-bearer,<br>Betwixt a goddess and a mighty [[archetypes/king\|king]].<br>Go, bid the drummer learn to touch the lute,<br>Or hang him in the braces of his drum;<br>For now we think it an uncivil thing<br>To trouble heaven with such harsh resounds. Away! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Lodowick |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | The quarrel that I have requires no arms<br>But these of mine, and these shall meet my foe<br>In a deep march of penetrable groans.<br>My eyes shall be my arrows, and my sighs<br>Shall serve me as the vantage of the [[motifs/wind\|wind]]<br>To whirl away my sweet’st artillery.<br>Ah, but alas, she wins the sun of me,<br>For that is she herself, and thence it comes<br>That poets term the wanton warrior blind.<br>But [[motifs/love\|love]] hath eyes as judgement to his steps,<br>Till too much loved glory dazzles them— |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lodowick |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | How now? |
| LODOWICK | My liege, the drum that struck the lusty march<br>Stands with Prince Edward, your thrice-valiant son. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Edward, Prince of Wales |
| *(didascalia)* | I see the boy. Aside O, how his mother’s face, |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Modelled in his, corrects my strayed [[motifs/desire\|desire]],<br>And rates my heart, and chides my thievish eye,<br>Who, being rich enough in seeing her,<br>Yet seek elsewhere; and basest theft is that<br>Which cannot cloak itself in [[concepts/poverty\|poverty]].<br>(To the Prince) Now, boy, what news? |
| PRINCE OF WALES | I have assembled, my dear lord and father,<br>The choicest buds of all our English [[motifs/blood\|blood]]<br>For our affairs to France, and here we come<br>To take direction from your majesty. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (aside)<br>Still do I see in him delineate<br>His mother’s visage. Those his eyes are hers,<br>Who looking wistly on me make me blush.<br>For faults against themselves give evidence;<br>Lust is a [[motifs/fire\|fire]], and men, like lanterns, show<br>Light lust within themselves, even through themselves.<br>Away, loose silks o’er wavering [[concepts/vanity\|vanity]]!<br>Shall the large limit of fair Brittany<br>By me be overthrown, and shall I not<br>Master this little mansion of myself?<br>Give me an armour of eternal steel:<br>I go to conquer kings; and shall I not then<br>Subdue myself and be my enemy’s friend?<br>It must not be. (To the Prince) Come, boy! Forward!<br>Advance!<br>Let’s with our colours sweet the air of France. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lodowick |
| LODOWICK | (to the [[archetypes/king\|King]])<br>My liege, the Countess, with a smiling cheer,<br>Desires access unto your majesty. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (aside)<br>Why there it goes. That very smile of hers<br>Hath ransomed captive France and set the [[archetypes/king\|King]],<br>The [[characters/dauphin\|Dauphin]] and the peers at liberty.<br>(To the Prince) Go, leave me, Ned, and revel with thy friends. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit the Prince of Wales |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (Aside) Thy mother is but black, and thou, like her,<br>Dost put it in my mind how foul she is.<br>(To Lodowick) Go, fetch the Countess hither in thy hand— |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Lodowick |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | And let her chase away these [[motifs/winter\|winter]] clouds,<br>For she gives [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] both to heaven and earth.<br>The sin is more to hack and hew poor men<br>Than to embrace in an unlawful bed<br>The register of all rarieties<br>Since leathern Adam till this youngest hour. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lodowick leading in by the hand the [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|Countess of Salisbury]] |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Go, Lod’wick, put thy hand into my purse—<br>Play, spend, give, riot, waste, do what thou wilt<br>So thou wilt hence awhile and leave me here. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Lodowick |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (To the Countess) Now, my [[motifs/soul\|soul]]’s playfellow, art thou come<br>To speak the more-than-heavenly word of ‘yea’<br>To my objection in thy beauteous [[motifs/love\|love]]? |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | My father on his blessing hath commanded— |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | That thou shalt yield to me. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Ay, dear my liege, your due. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | And that, my dearest [[motifs/love\|love]], can be no less<br>Than right for right, and render [[motifs/love\|love]] for [[motifs/love\|love]]. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Than wrong for wrong, and endless hate for hate.<br>But sith I see your majesty so bent<br>That my unwillingness, my husband’s [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>Your high estate, nor no respect respected<br>Can be my help, but that your mightiness<br>Will overbear and awe these dear regards,<br>I bind my discontent to thy content,<br>And what I would not ill compel I will,<br>Provided that yourself remove those lets<br>That stand between your highness’ [[motifs/love\|love]] and mine. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Name them, fair Countess, and by heaven I will. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | It is their lives that stand between our [[motifs/love\|love]]<br>That I would have choked up, my sovereign. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Whose lives, my lady? |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | My thrice-loving liege,<br>Your [[characters/queen\|Queen]] and Salisbury, my wedded husband,<br>Who, living, have that title in our [[motifs/love\|love]]<br>That we cannot bestow but by their [[motifs/death\|death]]. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Their opposition is beyond our law. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | So is your [[motifs/desire\|desire]]. If the law<br>Can hinder you to execute the one,<br>Let it forbid you to attempt the other.<br>I cannot think you [[motifs/love\|love]] me as you say<br>Unless you do make good what you have sworn. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | No more. Thy husband and the [[characters/queen\|Queen]] shall die.<br>Fairer thou art by far than [[archetypes/hero\|Hero]] was;<br>Beardless Leander not so strong as I.<br>He swam an easy current for his [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>But I will through a Hellespont of [[motifs/blood\|blood]]<br>To arrive at Sestos, where my [[archetypes/hero\|Hero]] lies. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Nay, you’ll do more. You’ll make the [[motifs/river\|river]] too<br>With their heart bloods that keep our [[motifs/love\|love]] asunder,<br>Of which my husband and your wife are twain. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Thy [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] makes them guilty of their [[motifs/death\|death]],<br>And gives in evidence that they shall die—<br>Upon which verdict I, their judge, condemn them. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | O, perjured [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]]! More corrupted judge!<br>When to the great star chamber o‘er our heads<br>The universal sessions calls to ’count<br>This packing evil, we both shall tremble for it. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | What says my fair [[motifs/love\|love]]? Is she resolute? |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Resolved to be dissolved, and therefore this:<br>Keep but thy word, great [[archetypes/king\|King]], and I am thine.<br>Stand where thou dost—I’ll part a little from thee—<br>She moves away from the [[archetypes/king\|King]] |
| *(didascalia)* | kneeling And see how I will yield me to thy hands. |
| [[characters/countess-of-salisbury\|COUNTESS OF SALISBURY]] | Here, by my side, doth hang my wedding knives.<br>She reveals two knives<br>Take thou the one,<br>She offers a knife to the [[archetypes/king\|King]]<br>and with it kill thy [[characters/queen\|Queen]],<br>And learn by me to find her where she lies;<br>And with this other<br>She turns the other knife on herself<br>I’ll dispatch my [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>Which now lies fast asleep within my heart.<br>When they are gone, then I’ll consent to [[motifs/love\|love]].<br>Stir not, lascivious [[archetypes/king\|King]], to hinder me.<br>My resolution is more nimbler far<br>Than thy prevention can be in my rescue.<br>An if thou stir, I strike. Therefore stand still,<br>And hear the choice that I will put thee to.<br>Either swear to leave thy most unholy suit<br>And never henceforth to solicit me,<br>Or else, by heaven, this sharp-pointed knife<br>Shall stain thy earth with that which thou wouldst stain—<br>My poor, chaste [[motifs/blood\|blood]]. Swear, Edward, swear,<br>Or I will strike and die before thee here. |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Even by that power I swear, that gives me now<br>The power to be ashamed of myself,<br>I never mean to part my lips again<br>In any words that tends to such a suit.<br>Arise, true English lady, whom our isle<br>May better boast of than ever Roman might<br>Of her, whose ransacked treasury hath tasked<br>The vain endeavour of so many pens.<br>Arise, and be my fault thy [[concepts/honour\|honour]]’s fame<br>Which after-ages shall enrich thee with.<br>I am awakèd from this idle dream. |
| *(didascalia)* | The Countess stands |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | (Calling) Warwick, my son, Derby, Artois and Audley—<br>Brave warriors all, where are you all this while? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter all the peers: the [[characters/earl-of-warwick\|Earl of Warwick]], the |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Prince of Wales, the Earl of Derby, the Comte d’Artois<br>and Lord Audley<br>Warwick, I make thee Warden of the North.<br>Thou, Prince of Wales, and Audley, straight to sea,<br>Scour to Newhaven—some there stay for me.<br>Myself, Artois and Derby will through Flanders<br>To greet our friends there and to crave their aid.<br>This [[motifs/night\|night]] will scarce suffice me to discover<br>My folly’s siege against a faithful [[archetypes/lover\|lover]],<br>For ere the sun shall gild the eastern sky<br>We’ll wake him with our martial harmony. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] EDWARD | Sc. 4 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Jean [[characters/king-of-france\|King of France]], his two sons (the [[characters/dauphin\|Dauphin]] and Prince Philippe) and the Duc de Lorraine |
| [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]] | Here, till our navy of a thousand sail<br>Have made a breakfast to our foe by sea,<br>Let us encamp to wait their happy [[characters/speed\|speed]].<br>Lorraine, what readiness is Edward in?<br>How hast thou heard that he provided is<br>Of martial furniture for this exploit? |
| DUC DE LORRAINE | To lay aside unnecessary soothing,<br>And not to spend the [[motifs/time\|time]] in circumstance,<br>’Tis bruited for a certainty, my lord,<br>That he’s exceeding strongly fortified.<br>His subjects flock as willingly to war<br>As if unto a triumph they were led. |
| [[characters/dauphin\|DAUPHIN]] | England was wont to harbour malcontents,<br>Bloodthirsty and seditious Catilines,<br>Spendthrifts, and such as gape for nothing else<br>But change and alteration of the state.<br>And is it possible<br>That they are now so loyal in themselves? |
| DUC DE LORRAINE | All but the Scot, who solemnly protests,<br>As heretofore I have informed his grace,<br>Never to sheathe his sword or take a truce. |
| [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]] | Ah, that’s the anch’rage of some better [[concepts/hope\|hope]].<br>But on the other side, to think what friends<br>[[archetypes/king\|King]] Edward hath retained in Netherland,<br>Among those ever-bibbing epicures—<br>Those frothy Dutchmen, puffed with [[topoi/double\|double]] beer,<br>That drink and swill in every place they come—<br>Doth not a little aggravate mine ire.<br>Besides, we hear the Emperor conjoins<br>And stalls him in his own authority.<br>But all the mightier that their number is<br>The greater glory reaps the victory!<br>Some friends have we beside domestic power—<br>The stern Polonian and the warlike Dane,<br>The [[archetypes/king\|King]] of Bohême, and of Sicily,<br>Are all become confederates with us<br>And, as I think, are marching hither apace— |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound drums within |
| [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]] | But soft, I hear the music of their drums,<br>By which I guess that their approach is near. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter at one door the [[archetypes/king\|King]] of Bohemia with Danish soldiers and a drummer. Enter at another door a Polish captain with Muscovite and Polish soldiers and a drummer |
| [[archetypes/king\|KING]] OF BOHEMIA | [[archetypes/king\|King]] Jean of France, as league and neighbourhood<br>Requires when friends are any way distressed,<br>I come to aid thee with my country’s force. |
| POLISH CAPTAIN | (to the [[characters/king-of-france\|King of France]])<br>And from great Moscow, fearful to the Turk,<br>And lofty Poland, [[characters/nurse\|nurse]] of hardy men,<br>I bring these servitors to fight for thee,<br>Who willingly will venture in thy cause. |
| [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]] | Welcome, Bohemian [[archetypes/king\|king]], and welcome all.<br>This your great kindness I will not forget.<br>Besides your plentiful rewards in crowns<br>That from our treasury ye shall receive,<br>There comes a harebrained nation, decked in [[concepts/pride\|pride]],<br>The spoil of whom will be a treble gain.<br>And now my [[concepts/hope\|hope]] is full, my joy complete.<br>At sea we are as puissant as the force<br>Of [[characters/agamemnon\|Agamemnon]] in the haven of Troy.<br>By land, with Xerxes we compare of strength,<br>Whose soldiers drank up rivers in their thirst.<br>Then, Bayard-like, blind overweening Ned,<br>To reach at our imperial diadem<br>Is either to be swallowed of the waves,<br>Or hacked a-pieces when thou com’st ashore. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a French Mariner |
| MARINER | Near to the coast I have descried, my lord,<br>As I was busy in my watchful charge,<br>The proud armada of [[archetypes/king\|King]] Edward’s ships,<br>Which, at the first far off when I did ken,<br>Seemed as it were a grove of withered pines,<br>But drawing near, their glorious bright aspect,<br>Their streaming ensigns wrought of coloured silk,<br>Like to a meadow full of sundry [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]],<br>Adorns the naked bosom of the earth.<br>Majestical the order of their course,<br>Figuring the hornèd circle of the [[motifs/moon\|moon]],<br>And on the top gallant of the admiral,<br>And likewise all the handmaids of his train,<br>The arms of England and of France unite<br>Are quartered equally by herald’s art.<br>Thus titely carried with a merry gale<br>They plough the ocean hitherward amain. |
| [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]] | Dare he already crop the fleur-de-lis?<br>I [[concepts/hope\|hope]], the honey being gathered thence,<br>He, with the spider, afterward approached,<br>Shall suck forth deadly venom from the [[motifs/leaves\|leaves]].<br>But where’s our navy? How are they prepared<br>To wing themselves against this flight of ravens? |
| MARINER | They, having knowledge brought them by the scouts,<br>Did break from anchor straight and, puffed with rage,<br>No otherwise than were their sails with [[motifs/wind\|wind]],<br>Made forth as when the empty eagle flies<br>To satisfy his hungry, griping maw. |
| [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]] | (giving money)<br>There’s for thy news. Return unto thy barque,<br>And if thou scape the bloody stroke of war<br>And do survive the conflict, come again,<br>And let us hear the manner of the fight. Exit Mariner<br>Mean space, my lords, ’tis best we be dispersed<br>To several places, lest they chance to land.<br>(To the [[archetypes/king\|King]] of Bohemia) First you, my lord, with your Bohemian troops,<br>Shall pitch your battles on the lower hand. |
| *(didascalia)* | (To the [[characters/dauphin\|Dauphin]] and the Polish captain) |
| [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]] | My eldest son, the Duke of Normandy,<br>Together with this aid of Muscovites,<br>Shall climb the higher ground another way.<br>Here in the middle coast, betwixt you both,<br>Philippe, my youngest boy, and I will lodge.<br>So, lords, be gone, and look unto your charge,<br>You stand for France, an empire fair and large. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but the [[characters/king-of-france\|King of France]] and Prince Philippe |
| [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]] | Now tell me, Philippe, what is thy conceit<br>Touching the challenge that the English make? |
| PRINCE PHILIPPE | I say, my lord, claim Edward what he can,<br>And bring he ne‘er so plain a pedigree,<br>’Tis you are in possession of the [[motifs/crown\|crown]],<br>And that’s the surest point of all the law.<br>But were it not, yet ere he should prevail<br>I’ll make a conduit of my dearest [[motifs/blood\|blood]],<br>Or chase those straggling upstarts [[motifs/home\|home]] again. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[motifs/well\|Well]] said, young Philippe! To an attendant Call for bread and wine |
| [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]] | That we may cheer our stomachs with repast<br>To look our foes more sternly in the face.<br>Bread and wine are brought forth. The battle is heard afar off. The [[archetypes/king\|King]] and Prince Philippe sup<br>Now is begun the heavy day at sea.<br>Fight, Frenchmen, fight! Be like the field of bears<br>When they defend their younglings in their caves.<br>Steer, angry Nemesis, the happy helm<br>That with the sulphur battles of your rage<br>The English fleet may be dispersed and sunk.<br>A cannon shot within |
| PRINCE PHILIPPE | O, father, how this echoing cannon shot,<br>Like sweet harmony, digests my cates! |
| [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]] | Now, boy, thou hear‘st what thund’ring terror ’tis<br>To buckle for a kingdom’s sovereignty.<br>The earth, with giddy trembling when it shakes,<br>Or when the exhalations of the air<br>Breaks in extremity of [[motifs/lightning\|lightning]] flash,<br>Affrights not more than kings when they dispose<br>To show the rancour of their high-swoll’n hearts. |
| *(didascalia)* | Retreat sounds within |
| *(didascalia)* | Retreat is sounded—one side hath the worse. |
| [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]] | O, if it be the French, sweet fortune turn,<br>And in thy turning, change the froward winds<br>That, with advantage of a favouring sky,<br>Our men may vanquish, and the other fly. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the French Mariner |
| [[characters/king-of-france\|KING OF FRANCE]] | My heart misgives. (To the Mariner) Say, [[motifs/mirror\|mirror]] of<br>pale [[motifs/death\|death]],<br>To whom belongs the [[concepts/honour\|honour]] of this day?<br>Relate, I pray thee, if thy breath will serve<br>The sad discourse of this discomfiture. |
| MARINER | I will, my lord.<br>My gracious sovereign, France hath ta‘en the foil,<br>And boasting Edward triumphs with success.<br>These iron-hearted navies,<br>When last I was reporter to your grace,<br>Both full of angry spleen, of [[concepts/hope\|hope]] and [[concepts/fear\|fear]],<br>Hasting to meet each other in the face,<br>At last conjoined, and by their admiral<br>Our admiral encountered many shot.<br>By this, the other, that beheld these twain<br>Give earnest-penny of a further wreck,<br>Like fiery dragons took their haughty flight;<br>And likewise meeting, from their smoky wombs<br>Sent many grim ambassadors of [[motifs/death\|death]].<br>Then ’gan the day to turn to gloomy [[motifs/night\|night]],<br>And darkness did as [[motifs/well\|well]] enclose the quick<br>As those that were but newly reft of life.<br>No leisure served for friends to bid farewell,<br>And if it had, the hideous noise was such<br>As each to other seemed deaf and dumb.<br>Purple the sea whose channel filled as fast<br>With streaming gore that from the maimed fell,<br>As did her gushing moisture break into<br>The cranny cleftures of the through-shot planks.<br>Here flew a head dissevered from the trunk;<br>There mangled arms and legs were tossed aloft,<br>As when a whirlwind takes the [[motifs/summer\|summer]] [[motifs/dust\|dust]]<br>And scatters it in middle of the air.<br>Then might ye see the reeling vessels split<br>And, tottering, sink into the ruthless flood<br>Until their lofty tops were seen no more.<br>All shifts were tried, both for defence and
